It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but especially when I’m supposed to sit quietly and do things like paperwork or go over shared household plans, I feel this internal restlessness, like I’m crawling out of my skin a little.\n\nIt’s not just that I don’t enjoy those tasks—nobody loves paperwork—but it feels like my body and mind won’t stay put long enough to get through them in a normal way. I’ll sit down intending to handle something simple, and almost immediately I’m tense and fidgety and uncomfortable, like I need to get up or move or do anything else. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should actually talk to someone instead of just continuing to be frustrated with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or urges to perform specific rituals that you feel compelled to follow?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way I understand rituals. I mean, I can get stuck thinking about things, but not like I have a specific thing I have to do over and over or something terrible will happen.\n\nI don’t have, like, a checking routine or counting or washing my hands a certain number of times—nothing like that comes to mind. If anything, I sometimes overthink whether I’m doing things “right,” but it doesn’t feel like a ritual I’m compelled to follow. It’s more just… my mind being busy and hard to quiet, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations where you're not able to move around or be active?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the main things that made me come in. When I’m in a situation where I’m supposed to just sit still and quietly focus—like doing paperwork, going through bills or forms, or even sitting down to talk through household plans with someone—I start feeling really on edge.\n\nIt’s not always “anxious” in the sense of being scared of something specific. It’s more like this pressure in my body, like I can’t settle. I’ll think, okay, just sit here and do this one thing, it’s not a big deal, but then almost right away I feel uncomfortable and restless, like I need to shift around or get up or just not be trapped in that stillness. It makes those ordinary tasks feel much harder than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have more energy than you know what to do with, especially when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, actually. It does feel like I have this extra energy that doesn’t really have anywhere to go, especially if I’m supposed to be still.\n\nLike on days when I’m home, I’ll tell myself I’m going to do one thing, but then I’m moving from laundry to emails to reorganizing some random drawer to checking the calendar, and I’m not really taking an actual break between any of it. It’s not even always productive in a satisfying way—it’s more like I’m being pushed from the inside to keep doing the next thing, and the next thing, and the next thing.\n\nAnd then if I’m expected to sit still, that energy feels even louder. It’s like my body doesn’t understand that “resting” or “just sitting” is what’s supposed to be happening.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms affecting your relationships with family and friends or your ability to work or go to school?\n\nPatient: Yes… it is, and honestly that’s probably the part I feel the most ashamed about. It’s not just an internal thing where I’m frustrated with myself. It does spill over onto other people.\n\nAt home, people can’t always count on me the way I want them to. I’ll fully intend to follow through on something, and I really mean it when I say I’m going to do it, but then something slips through or I get off track, and it ends up affecting everyone else. And then I’m apologizing again, or trying to explain that I wasn’t being careless on purpose, but I know after a while explanations don’t really fix the fact that the thing didn’t get done.\n\nIt’s happened in volunteer things too. I’m not working right now, but I do try to be involved, and I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because I just wasn’t reliable enough. Not because I didn’t care—if anything I cared a lot—but I kept dropping pieces of things despite really trying hard to keep up. And that feels awful, because I don’t want people to see me as flaky or irresponsible, but I can understand why they might.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting relationships and responsibilities. It’s like the fallout is that people around me have learned they can’t always depend on me, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly in conversation, I think. I don’t usually think of myself as someone who’s trying to be rude or reckless, and I really do care a lot about being respectful, but I catch myself jumping in before I’ve actually waited my turn.\n\nLike with my husband, especially, if he’s telling me something or talking through an idea, I’ll suddenly interrupt because the response is already right there in my mouth. It’s like I hear the first part and my brain finishes the rest, and then I’m answering before he’s even done. Sometimes I’ll even finish his sentence or try to complete his thought, and I can tell afterward that it must feel intrusive or like I’m not really listening, even though in my head I’m very engaged.\n\nThe frustrating thing is I often realize it a split second too late. I’ll think, “Why did I just cut him off? Just wait.” But by then I’ve already done it. So yes, I do act before fully thinking it through in that way, and then I feel embarrassed or guilty afterward because that’s not how I want to come across.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to settle in most areas of your life, or is it mainly in specific situations?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s pretty broad, honestly. It’s not only one specific place, although it definitely gets worse in situations where I’m supposed to sit quietly and just deal with something boring or structured.\n\nLike paperwork is a big one. If I have forms to fill out, bills to sort, anything where I’m supposed to sit at the table and calmly go through it step by step, I feel like I can’t settle in my own body. I’ll shift around, get up, come back, look for a pen, suddenly need water, then I’m standing there thinking, “Why am I standing up? I was supposed to be doing the paperwork.” It feels almost physically uncomfortable to stay with it.\n\nAnd shared household planning is another big one—like sitting down with my husband to go over schedules or decisions or practical things we need to coordinate. I want to be present for that, because it matters, but inside I’m restless. I feel like I’m trying to hold myself in place. So it’s not just, “Oh, I’m bored.” It’s more like my body and brain are pushing against the idea of staying still and calmly handling the thing in front of me.\n\nSo yes, it shows up in a lot of areas, but especially when I’m expected to be still, quiet, organized, and patient with details. That’s when it becomes really noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… when I look back, I think it was there really early. I don’t think I had the words for it then, of course, and I probably just thought I was “messy” or “talkative” or “bad at being organized,” but in elementary school it was already showing up.\n\nI was that girl with the disaster of a desk—papers shoved everywhere, things crumpled up, stuff I was supposed to take home still sitting there days later. Permission slips were a whole thing too. I would forget them, lose them, or remember them at the last possible second, and then it became this panic at home or at school. And I remember teachers sending notes or making comments about me talking out of turn, like blurting things out before I was supposed to, or seeming like I couldn’t just stay seated and settled the way other kids could.\n\nIt’s a little painful to think about now, because at the time I think it got framed as me not trying hard enough, or being careless, but I really don’t remember it feeling like a choice. I remember wanting to do the right thing and still somehow ending up with the messy desk, the missing papers, the teacher reminders. So yes, I’d say these patterns go back to childhood, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to pay attention to details or make careless mistakes, compared to feeling restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, the detail mistakes are a big part of it too. The restlessness is more obvious from the outside, I think, because people can see me shifting or getting up or interrupting myself, but the detail thing causes a lot of problems in a quieter way.\n\nLike, I’ll be putting together something simple—well, “simple”—like a volunteer sign-up sheet, and I genuinely think I’m being careful. I’ll look it over, maybe even more than once, and then later someone will point out that I put the wrong date on it, or I skipped a line so people’s names don’t match the right time slot, or I copied something into the wrong place. And it’s embarrassing because it looks like I rushed or didn’t care, but in my mind I did check it.\n\nIt happens with our household budget too. I’ll enter numbers and think, “Okay, I’m paying attention, I’ve got this,” and then later realize I reversed digits or put an amount in the wrong category, or missed a line entirely. Those little mistakes can make the whole thing not add up, and then I have to go back through it all, which is frustrating because I was trying so hard to avoid exactly that.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say it’s harder than the restlessness, but it’s definitely just as real. The fidgety feeling is uncomfortable, but the careless-detail mistakes are the part that make me feel kind of ashamed, because I thought I had caught them and then I find out I didn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had problems in your relationships, or been unable to fulfill responsibilities because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I haven’t been fired from a job recently or anything like that, but I’m not working right now, so the place it shows up most is at home and in volunteer things. And honestly, the hardest part is that people can’t always count on me the way I want them to.\n\nAt home, it’s like I’ll genuinely mean to handle something, and I’ll tell my husband, “Yes, I’ve got it,” and then somehow it doesn’t get done, or it gets partly done, or I miss an important piece of it. And then it creates this ripple effect where he has to check behind me or remind me, which feels awful because I don’t want to be managed like a child, but I also understand why he gets frustrated. It affects trust, not in a dramatic way like some huge betrayal, but in that day-to-day reliability way.\n\nAnd with volunteer responsibilities, it’s been similar. I’ve had roles where I was supposed to coordinate things or keep track of details, and I would try really hard, but things would fall through. I’d miss something, forget to follow up, not get something sent out when I said I would, or realize too late that I hadn’t finished a piece of it. After enough of that, I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because I could feel that I was becoming someone people had to work around.\n\nThat’s probably the most painful part—because I care. I’m not shrugging it off. I’m trying, and I still end up letting people down. So yes, it has definitely caused problems with responsibilities and with how reliable I seem to people close to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is with paying attention to details and following through on tasks, or is it more with feeling restless and acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, what brings me in most is the restless part. The details and follow-through are definitely a problem, and I don’t want to minimize that, because it affects real things. But the thing that feels most unbearable lately is that I can’t seem to settle in my own body.\n\nEspecially when I’m supposed to sit quietly and do something like paperwork, or go over household plans with my husband, or look at schedules and decisions together. It’s like I know I need to stay with it, I know it matters, but inside I’m already crawling out of my skin. I’ll shift around, feel this pressure to get up or move or escape the task somehow, even if I’m trying to look calm on the outside.\n\nSo if I had to choose, I’d say the restlessness is the thing that feels most urgent and distressing. The attention-to-detail problems cause consequences, but the “I cannot sit here and be still and deal with this” feeling is what made me feel like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and impulsivity are more bothersome to you than your difficulties with paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are more bothersome to me, at least right now. The attention-to-detail problems are upsetting, definitely, but what really brings me in is this feeling like I can’t settle in my own body anymore.\n\nIt’s especially bad when I’m supposed to sit quietly and handle something that needs focus, like paperwork or going over household plans with my husband. I’ll know it’s important, and I’ll want to stay present, but inside I feel like I’m almost buzzing or trapped. Like I need to get up, move, stop the conversation, do anything other than just sit there and work through it calmly.\n\nSo yes… the detail issues cause problems, but the restlessness is the part that feels most uncomfortable and hard to live with day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with paying attention to details or your feelings of restlessness and acting on impulse cause you more problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I think the restlessness causes me more problems day to day, even though the detail stuff absolutely matters too. But what really brings me in is that I feel like I can’t settle in my own body anymore.\n\nIt’s especially noticeable when I’m supposed to sit quietly and handle things like paperwork, or when my husband and I are trying to go over shared household plans. Those aren’t dramatic situations, but they feel almost impossible sometimes. I’ll be sitting there knowing, “Okay, this needs to get done, this is important, I need to stay with this,” but inside I feel this pressure building, like I need to move or get away from it. It’s not just boredom exactly—it’s more like my body won’t cooperate with staying still and working through it.\n\nSo if I’m choosing which one causes more trouble, I’d say the restlessness, because it makes ordinary responsibilities feel so hard to even remain present for.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity in most social situations, or are they mainly limited to specific contexts like work or home?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly in specific situations, I think. I mean, I can feel kind of unsettled in different places, but what really brings me in is that feeling like I can’t settle in my own body when I’m supposed to sit quietly and deal with something.\n\nThe clearest examples are paperwork or when my husband and I are going over household plans together—like schedules, decisions, things we need to sort through. Those situations are not exactly social in the casual sense, but they require me to sit there, stay present, listen, respond, and not just escape the whole thing. And that’s where it gets really uncomfortable. I feel trapped in my own skin, almost like I need to get up or move or make it stop.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s every social situation across the board. It’s more when I’m expected to be still and focused, especially with home responsibilities or paperwork-type things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes, very easily. That’s actually one of the things that makes simple tasks take forever for me.\n\nLike if I’m sitting at the kitchen table trying to work on something—paperwork, paying a bill, filling something out—I’ll think I’m focused, and then my phone buzzes, or I hear a car go by outside, or even just some random thought pops in like, “What are we doing for dinner?” And before I even realize what happened, I’m not doing the thing anymore. I’m looking at my phone, or staring out the window, or mentally planning a grocery list, and then I have to sort of come back and figure out where I was.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it doesn’t always feel like I made a choice to stop. It’s more like my attention gets yanked away, and then I only notice after I’ve already lost the thread.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with tasks start before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Looking back, it really does seem like this was already part of me in elementary school, even if nobody framed it that way at the time.\n\nI was kind of the girl with the messy desk all the time—papers shoved everywhere, things half-crumpled, stuff I was supposed to bring home just disappearing into the bottom of the desk or my backpack. I remember forgotten permission slips being a recurring thing, like I’d have them, and then somehow they just wouldn’t make it to my parents or back to school when they were supposed to.\n\nAnd there were definitely teacher notes, not every single day maybe, but enough that I remember it, saying I talked out of turn or that I had trouble staying seated. I can picture that feeling of wanting to pop up or turn around and say something before I was supposed to. So yes, before 12, definitely. It wasn’t new in adulthood.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness or difficulty with tasks has significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s probably the part that feels the most painful, honestly, because it’s not just “oh, I’m a little scattered” or “I had a busy week.” There’s been real fallout from it.\n\nAt home, it affects whether people can count on me. My husband will ask me to handle something, or I’ll agree to take care of a piece of some plan, and I really do mean it when I say yes. I’m not being careless on purpose. But then things slip, or I don’t follow through in the way I said I would, or I’m late getting back to something, and over time that wears on people. It makes me feel like I’m constantly apologizing for the same kind of thing.\n\nAnd it’s happened in volunteer circles too. I’ve had responsibilities where I wanted to be dependable, and I’d try hard, make lists, tell myself I was going to stay on top of it, but things would still fall through. Eventually I’ve had to step back from some roles because I could tell people couldn’t reliably count on me, and honestly I couldn’t always count on myself either.\n\nSo yes, it has affected my daily life and relationships. Not because I don’t care—if anything I care a lot—but because the follow-through just doesn’t match my intentions.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with tasks for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely more than six months. The noticeable worsening has been since I got married and our routines sort of merged together, which was about… a year and five months ago now.\n\nI mean, I know I’ve had versions of this for longer, but in terms of it becoming really obvious day-to-day, that’s the timeframe that stands out. Once it wasn’t just my own schedule and my own clutter or my own way of doing things, and there was another person’s routine intertwined with mine, it became much harder to ignore. So yes, it’s been well over six months.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to pay attention to details or find it hard to maintain focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things I notice a lot, and it’s frustrating because I can start out with every intention of paying attention.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down with a planning email—something I actually need to read carefully because there are dates or instructions or details in it—and at first I’m doing okay. I’ll read the first few lines and think, okay, I’m focused, I’m doing this. But then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been looking at the same section over and over, or my eyes are moving across the words but none of it is really going in. And then I have to start again, sometimes more than once.\n\nIt happens with books too. I’ll read a chapter, or try to, and then realize I don’t really know what I just read. It’s not that I can’t understand it. It’s more like my attention just slips away without me noticing, and then suddenly I’m staring at the same paragraph like, wait, what did that say? And then I feel annoyed with myself because it seems like such a basic thing to just sit and read or stay with one task, but it takes a lot more effort than it looks like it should.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impaired your ability to function socially or occupationally?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I’m not working right now, so I can’t speak to a current job situation, but in terms of functioning in everyday roles and responsibilities, it’s definitely impaired things.\n\nThe main fallout has been that people at home can’t always reliably count on me, even when I really want them to be able to. I was supposed to be doing the paperwork.” It feels almost physically uncomfortable to stay with it.\n\nAnd shared household planning is another big one—like sitting down with my husband to go over schedules or decisions or practical things we need to coordinate. So it’s not just, “Oh, I’m bored.” It’s more like my body and brain are pushing against the idea of staying still and calmly handling the thing in front of me.\n\nSo yes, it shows up in a lot of areas, but especially when I’m expected to be still, quiet, organized, and patient with details. That’s when it becomes really noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… when I look back, I think it was there really early. I don’t think I had the words for it then, of course, and I probably just thought I was “messy” or “talkative” or “bad at being organized,” but in elementary school it was already showing up.\n\nI was that girl with the disaster of a desk—papers shoved everywhere, things crumpled up, stuff I was supposed to take home still sitting there days later. Permission slips were a whole thing too. I would forget them, lose them, or remember them at the last possible second, and then it became this panic at home or at school. And I remember teachers sending notes or making comments about me talking out of turn, like blurting things out before I was supposed to, or seeming like I couldn’t just stay seated and settled the way other kids could.\n\nIt’s a little painful to think about now, because at the time I think it got framed as me not trying hard enough, or being careless, but I really don’t remember it feeling like a choice. I remember wanting to do the right thing and still somehow ending up with the messy desk, the missing papers, the teacher reminders. So yes, I’d say these patterns go back to childhood, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to pay attention to details or make careless mistakes, compared to feeling restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, the detail mistakes are a big part of it too. The restlessness is more obvious from the outside, I think, because people can see me shifting or getting up or interrupting myself, but the detail thing causes a lot of problems in a quieter way.\n\nLike, I’ll be putting together something simple—well, “simple”—like a volunteer sign-up sheet, and I genuinely think I’m being careful. I’ll look it over, maybe even more than once, and then later someone will point out that I put the wrong date on it, or I skipped a line so people’s names don’t match the right time slot, or I copied something into the wrong place. And it’s embarrassing because it looks like I rushed or didn’t care, but in my mind I did check it.\n\nIt happens with our household budget too. I’ll enter numbers and think, “Okay, I’m paying attention, I’ve got this,” and then later realize I reversed digits or put an amount in the wrong category, or missed a line entirely. Those little mistakes can make the whole thing not add up, and then I have to go back through it all, which is frustrating because I was trying so hard to avoid exactly that.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say it’s harder than the restlessness, but it’s definitely just as real. The fidgety feeling is uncomfortable, but the careless-detail mistakes are the part that make me feel kind of ashamed, because I thought I had caught them and then I find out I didn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had problems in your relationships, or been unable to fulfill responsibilities because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I haven’t been fired from a job recently or anything like that, but I’m not working right now, so the place it shows up most is at home and in volunteer things. And honestly, the hardest part is that people can’t always count on me the way I want them to.\n\nAt home, it’s like I’ll genuinely mean to handle something, and I’ll tell my husband, “Yes, I’ve got it,” and then somehow it doesn’t get done, or it gets partly done, or I miss an important piece of it. And then it creates this ripple effect where he has to check behind me or remind me, which feels awful because I don’t want to be managed like a child, but I also understand why he gets frustrated. It affects trust, not in a dramatic way like some huge betrayal, but in that day-to-day reliability way.\n\nAnd with volunteer responsibilities, it’s been similar. I’ve had roles where I was supposed to coordinate things or keep track of details, and I would try really hard, but things would fall through. I’d miss something, forget to follow up, not get something sent out when I said I would, or realize too late that I hadn’t finished a piece of it. After enough of that, I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because I could feel that I was becoming someone people had to work around.\n\nThat’s probably the most painful part—because I care. I’m not shrugging it off. I’m trying, and I still end up letting people down. So yes, it has definitely caused problems with responsibilities and with how reliable I seem to people close to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is with paying attention to details and following through on tasks, or is it more with feeling restless and acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, what brings me in most is the restless part. The details and follow-through are definitely a problem, and I don’t want to minimize that, because it affects real things. But the thing that feels most unbearable lately is that I can’t seem to settle in my own body.\n\nEspecially when I’m supposed to sit quietly and do something like paperwork, or go over household plans with my husband, or look at schedules and decisions together. It’s like I know I need to stay with it, I know it matters, but inside I’m already crawling out of my skin. I’ll shift around, feel this pressure to get up or move or escape the task somehow, even if I’m trying to look calm on the outside.\n\nSo if I had to choose, I’d say the restlessness is the thing that feels most urgent and distressing. The attention-to-detail problems cause consequences, but the “I cannot sit here and be still and deal with this” feeling is what made me feel like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and impulsivity are more bothersome to you than your difficulties with paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they are more bothersome to me, at least right now. The attention-to-detail problems are upsetting, definitely, but what really brings me in is this feeling like I can’t settle in my own body anymore.\n\nIt’s especially bad when I’m supposed to sit quietly and handle something that needs focus, like paperwork or going over household plans with my husband. I’ll know it’s important, and I’ll want to stay present, but inside I feel like I’m almost buzzing or trapped. Like I need to get up, move, stop the conversation, do anything other than just sit there and work through it calmly.\n\nSo yes… the detail issues cause problems, but the restlessness is the part that feels most uncomfortable and hard to live with day to day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with paying attention to details or your feelings of restlessness and acting on impulse cause you more problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I think the restlessness causes me more problems day to day, even though the detail stuff absolutely matters too. But what really brings me in is that I feel like I can’t settle in my own body anymore.\n\nIt’s especially noticeable when I’m supposed to sit quietly and handle things like paperwork, or when my husband and I are trying to go over shared household plans. Those aren’t dramatic situations, but they feel almost impossible sometimes. I’ll be sitting there knowing, “Okay, this needs to get done, this is important, I need to stay with this,” but inside I feel this pressure building, like I need to move or get away from it. It’s not just boredom exactly—it’s more like my body won’t cooperate with staying still and working through it.\n\nSo if I’m choosing which one causes more trouble, I’d say the restlessness, because it makes ordinary responsibilities feel so hard to even remain present for.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity in most social situations, or are they mainly limited to specific contexts like work or home?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly in specific situations, I think. I mean, I can feel kind of unsettled in different places, but what really brings me in is that feeling like I can’t settle in my own body when I’m supposed to sit quietly and deal with something.\n\nThe clearest examples are paperwork or when my husband and I are going over household plans together—like schedules, decisions, things we need to sort through. Those situations are not exactly social in the casual sense, but they require me to sit there, stay present, listen, respond, and not just escape the whole thing. And that’s where it gets really uncomfortable. I feel trapped in my own skin, almost like I need to get up or move or make it stop.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s every social situation across the board. It’s more when I’m expected to be still and focused, especially with home responsibilities or paperwork-type things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes, very easily. That’s actually one of the things that makes simple tasks take forever for me.\n\nLike if I’m sitting at the kitchen table trying to work on something—paperwork, paying a bill, filling something out—I’ll think I’m focused, and then my phone buzzes, or I hear a car go by outside, or even just some random thought pops in like, “What are we doing for dinner?” And before I even realize what happened, I’m not doing the thing anymore. I’m looking at my phone, or staring out the window, or mentally planning a grocery list, and then I have to sort of come back and figure out where I was.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it doesn’t always feel like I made a choice to stop. It’s more like my attention gets yanked away, and then I only notice after I’ve already lost the thread.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with tasks start before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Looking back, it really does seem like this was already part of me in elementary school, even if nobody framed it that way at the time.\n\nI was kind of the girl with the messy desk all the time—papers shoved everywhere, things half-crumpled, stuff I was supposed to bring home just disappearing into the bottom of the desk or my backpack. I remember forgotten permission slips being a recurring thing, like I’d have them, and then somehow they just wouldn’t make it to my parents or back to school when they were supposed to.\n\nAnd there were definitely teacher notes, not every single day maybe, but enough that I remember it, saying I talked out of turn or that I had trouble staying seated. I can picture that feeling of wanting to pop up or turn around and say something before I was supposed to. So yes, before 12, definitely. It wasn’t new in adulthood.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness or difficulty with tasks has significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s probably the part that feels the most painful, honestly, because it’s not just “oh, I’m a little scattered” or “I had a busy week.” There’s been real fallout from it.\n\nAt home, it affects whether people can count on me. My husband will ask me to handle something, or I’ll agree to take care of a piece of some plan, and I really do mean it when I say yes. I’m not being careless on purpose. But then things slip, or I don’t follow through in the way I said I would, or I’m late getting back to something, and over time that wears on people. It makes me feel like I’m constantly apologizing for the same kind of thing.\n\nAnd it’s happened in volunteer circles too. I’ve had responsibilities where I wanted to be dependable, and I’d try hard, make lists, tell myself I was going to stay on top of it, but things would still fall through. Eventually I’ve had to step back from some roles because I could tell people couldn’t reliably count on me, and honestly I couldn’t always count on myself either.\n\nSo yes, it has affected my daily life and relationships. Not because I don’t care—if anything I care a lot—but because the follow-through just doesn’t match my intentions.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with tasks for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely more than six months. The noticeable worsening has been since I got married and our routines sort of merged together, which was about… a year and five months ago now.\n\nI mean, I know I’ve had versions of this for longer, but in terms of it becoming really obvious day-to-day, that’s the timeframe that stands out. Once it wasn’t just my own schedule and my own clutter or my own way of doing things, and there was another person’s routine intertwined with mine, it became much harder to ignore. So yes, it’s been well over six months.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to pay attention to details or find it hard to maintain focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things I notice a lot, and it’s frustrating because I can start out with every intention of paying attention.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down with a planning email—something I actually need to read carefully because there are dates or instructions or details in it—and at first I’m doing okay. I’ll read the first few lines and think, okay, I’m focused, I’m doing this. But then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been looking at the same section over and over, or my eyes are moving across the words but none of it is really going in. And then I have to start again, sometimes more than once.\n\nIt happens with books too. I’ll read a chapter, or try to, and then realize I don’t really know what I just read. It’s not that I can’t understand it. It’s more like my attention just slips away without me noticing, and then suddenly I’m staring at the same paragraph like, wait, what did that say? And then I feel annoyed with myself because it seems like such a basic thing to just sit and read or stay with one task, but it takes a lot more effort than it looks like it should.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impaired your ability to function socially or occupationally?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I’m not working right now, so I can’t speak to a current job situation, but in terms of functioning in everyday roles and responsibilities, it’s definitely impaired things.\n\nThe main fallout has been that people at home can’t always reliably count on me, even when I really want them to be able to. I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and I mean it at the time, but then it falls through or gets delayed or I forget some piece of it, and then someone else has to follow up or pick up the slack. That creates tension, and I feel awful because it looks like I don’t care or I’m being irresponsible, when from my side it feels like I’m trying and still not managing it consistently.\n\nAnd socially, or maybe more in community settings, it’s shown up in volunteer roles too. I’ve had responsibilities where I genuinely wanted to contribute and be dependable, but I kept dropping the ball despite making lists or trying to be more careful. Eventually I’ve had to step back from some of those responsibilities because I could tell people couldn’t count on me the way they needed to, and honestly I didn’t trust myself to keep letting people down.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected my ability to function, especially in maintaining responsibilities and trust with other people."
